Sign-changing blow-up for the Moser–Trudinger equation Thanks: The first author was supported by the Swiss National Foundation Grants PP00P2-144669 and PP00P2-170588/1. The second author was supported by the French ANR Grant BLADE-JC. The third author was supported by the Discovery Grant RGPIN-2016-04195 from the Natural Sciences and Engineering Research Council of Canada.
Luca Martinazzi, Pierre-Damien Thizy, Jérôme Vétois

Abstract
. Given a sufficiently symmetric domain Ω ⋐ ℝ 2 \Omega\Subset\mathbb{R}^{2} , for any k ∈ ℕ ∖ { 0 } k\in\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\} and β > 4 ​ π ​ k \beta>4\pi k we construct blowing-up solutions ( u ε ) ⊂ H 0 1 ​ ( Ω ) \left(u_{\varepsilon}\right)\subset H^{1}_{0}\left(\Omega\right) to the Moser–Trudinger equation such that as ε ↓ 0 \varepsilon\downarrow 0 , we have ‖ ∇ u ε ‖ L 2 2 → β \left\|\nabla u_{\varepsilon}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\to\beta , u ε ⇀ u 0 u_{\varepsilon}\rightharpoonup u_{0} in H 0 1 H^{1}_{0} where u 0 u_{0} is a sign-changing solution of the Moser–Trudinger equation and u ε u_{\varepsilon} develops k k positive spherical bubbles, all concentrating at 0 ∈ Ω 0\in\Omega . These 3 3 features (lack of quantization, non-zero weak limit and bubble clustering) stand in sharp contrast to the positive case ( u ε > 0 u_{\varepsilon}>0 ) studied by the second author and Druet [ DruThi ] .



1.
Introduction and main result

Given a smooth, bounded domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
and a smooth, positive function
h
on
\overline{\Omega}
, we consider the Moser–Trudinger functional
I_{h}:H^{1}_{0}\left(\Omega\right)\to\mathbb{R}
defined as

I_{h}\left(u\right):=\int_{\Omega}h\exp\left(u^{2}\right)dx\qquad\forall u\in H^{1}_{0}\left(\Omega\right).

For any
\beta>0
, let
E_{h,\beta}
be the set of all the critical points
u\in H^{1}_{0}\left(\Omega\right)
of
I_{h}
under the constraint
\left\|\nabla u\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\beta
. Note that
u\in E_{h,\beta}
if and only if
u
is a solution of the problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta u=\lambda hf\left(u\right)&&\text{in }\Omega\\
&u=0&&\text{on }\partial\Omega,\end{aligned}\right.

(
\mathcal{E}_{h,\beta}
)

where we use the notation
\Delta:=-\partial^{2}_{x_{1}}-\partial^{2}_{x_{2}}
,

f\left(u\right):=u\exp\left(u^{2}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda:=\frac{2\beta}{DI_{h}\left(u\right).u}=\frac{\beta}{\int_{\Omega}hu^{2}\exp\left(u^{2}\right)dx}\,.

(1.1)

We first introduce the following definition in the spirit of
[
ZurichBook
]
*Chapter 5 (see also Remark
4.8
):

Definition 1.1
.

We say that
\beta>0
is a
stable energy level of
I_{h}
if, for all
(h_{\varepsilon})
,
(\beta_{\varepsilon})
and
(\lambda_{\varepsilon})
such that
h_{\varepsilon}\to h
in
C^{2}(\bar{\Omega})
and
\beta_{\varepsilon}\to\beta
with
\lambda_{\varepsilon}=O(1)
, any family
(u_{\varepsilon})
such that
u_{\varepsilon}
solves
(\mathcal{E}_{h_{\varepsilon},\beta_{\varepsilon}})
with
\lambda=\lambda_{\varepsilon}
for all
\varepsilon
converges in
C^{2}(\bar{\Omega})
to some
u
solving (

E

h
,
β

) as
\varepsilon\to 0
, up to a subsequence. We say that
\beta>0
is a
positively stable energy level of
I_{h}
if the same holds true with
u_{\varepsilon}\geq 0
.

As a consequence of the Moser–Trudinger inequality
[
Mos
,
Tru
]
, every
\beta\in\left(0,4\pi\right)
is a stable energy level of
I_{h}
. Druet–Thizy
[
DruThi
]
obtained that every
\beta\in\left(0,\infty\right)\backslash 4\pi\mathbb{N}^{\star}
(
\mathbb{N}^{\star}:=\mathbb{N}\setminus\{0\}
) is a positively stable energy level of
I_{h}
. In contrast to this result, we obtain in this paper that every
\beta\geq 4\pi
is an unstable energy level provided
\Omega
and
h
are such that
0\in\Omega
and the following symmetric condition holds true for some even number
l\in 2\mathbb{N}^{\star}
:

(A)

\Omega
is symmetric and
h
is even with respect to the lines

\ell_{j}:=\left\{\left(t\cos\left(\frac{j\pi}{2l}\right),t\sin\left(\frac{j\pi}{2l}\right)\right):\,t\in\mathbb{R}\right\},\quad 0\leq j\leq 2l-1.

Under this assumption, we obtain the following:

Theorem 1.2
.

Let
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a smooth, bounded domain,
l\in 2\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
\alpha\in\left(0,1\right)
and
h\in C^{l-2,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{2}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
be a positive function such that
0\in\Omega
and (A) holds true. Then every
\beta\geq 4\pi
is an unstable energy level of
I_{h}
.

In order to prove Theorem
1.2
we will construct a sign-changing weak limit
w_{0}
with arbitrary energy
\beta_{0}\in(0,8\pi l)
and use a Lyapunov-Schmidt procedure to glue to
w_{0}
an arbitrary number
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
of bubbles, all concentrating at the origin. This is in sharp contrast to the positive case studied by Druet–Thizy
[
DruThi
]
, in which blow-up can happen only at energy levels
\beta\in 4\pi\mathbb{N}^{\star}
, the weak limit vanishes and the bubbles blow up at distinct points. See also
[
delPinoMusRuf1
,
delPinoMusRuf2
,
DruMalMarThi
]
for the constructive counterpart of
[
DruThi
]
.

To be more concrete, given
h\in C^{l-2,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{2}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
and
\beta_{0}>0
, using the symmetry of
\Omega
and
h
, we will construct
w_{0}\in E_{h,\beta_{0}}
such that

w_{0}\left(x_{1},0\right)\sim a_{0}x_{1}^{l},\quad\text{as }x_{1}\to 0,\quad\text{for some }a_{0}>0.

(1.2)

Up to a perturbation and a diagonal argument, we can assume that
w_{0}
is non degenerate, and construct families
h_{\varepsilon}\to h
in
C^{2}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
,
\beta_{\varepsilon}\to\beta_{0}
and
w_{\varepsilon}
,
0\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}
, smooth with respect to
\varepsilon
such that
w_{\varepsilon}\in E_{h_{\varepsilon},\beta_{\varepsilon}}
and
0>w_{\varepsilon}\left(0\right)\uparrow 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
.
The behaviour (
1.2
) of the weak limit
w_{0}
near the origin will be crucial to glue bubbles and the value of
w_{\varepsilon}\left(0\right)\uparrow 0
will determine the parameter
\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\to\infty
(see (
3.6
)), which is the approximate height of the bubbles.

In fact, if
\overline{B}_{\gamma}
is the radial solution to
\Delta\overline{B}_{\gamma}=f\big(\overline{B}_{\gamma}\big)
with
\overline{B}_{\gamma}\left(0\right)=\gamma
, we will attach to the function
w_{\varepsilon}
a fixed number
k
of perturbations of
\overline{B}_{\gamma_{\varepsilon}}
along the
x_{1}
axis, at points
\left(\tau_{\varepsilon,1},0\right),\dots,\left(\tau_{\varepsilon,k},0\right)
. The centers
(\tau_{\varepsilon,i},0)
of the bubbles will satisfy for some
\delta\in(0,1)
,

-\frac{kd_{\varepsilon}}{\delta}<\tau_{\varepsilon,1}<\dots<\tau_{\varepsilon,k}<\frac{kd_{\varepsilon}}{\delta},\quad|\tau_{\varepsilon,i}-\tau_{\varepsilon,j}|>\delta d_{\varepsilon},\quad d_{\varepsilon}:=\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{-1/l}\to 0,

(1.3)

and, up to scaling,
\left(\tau_{\varepsilon,1}/d_{\varepsilon},\dots,\tau_{\varepsilon,k}/d_{\varepsilon}\right)
will converge to a zero of
N=(N^{1},\dots,N^{k})
, defined in a suitable convex subset of
\mathbb{R}^{k}
as

N^{i}\left(y_{1},\dots,y_{k}\right):=a_{0}ly_{i}^{l-1}-\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{2}{y_{i}-y_{j}}.

(1.4)

Note that, contrary to the case studied in
[
delPinoMusRuf1
,
delPinoMusRuf2
,
DruThi
]
, the function
h
(more specifically, its gradient) plays no role in (
1.4
), hence at main order it does not influence the location of the bubbles, which instead depends on
a_{0}>0
and
l
as in (
1.2
) and on
k
.

A diagonal argument allows to treat the case
\beta_{0}=0
. Thus we finally obtain:

Theorem 1.3
.

Given
\Omega
,
l
,
\alpha
and
h
as in Theorem
1.2
and
\beta\geq 4\pi
,
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
\beta_{0}\geq 0
such that
\beta=\beta_{0}+4\pi k
, there exist
w_{0}\in E_{h,\beta_{0}}
and
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for every
\varepsilon\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})
, we can find
h_{\varepsilon}\to h
in
C^{2}(\overline{\Omega})
,
\beta^{\prime}_{\varepsilon}\to\beta_{0}
, as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
w_{\varepsilon}\in E_{h_{\varepsilon},\beta_{\varepsilon}^{\prime}}
as in (
1.2
), numbers
\beta_{\varepsilon}\to\beta
,
\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\to\infty
,
\gamma_{\varepsilon}
,
\tau_{\varepsilon}
,
\theta_{\varepsilon}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
, with
\gamma_{\varepsilon,i}\sim\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}
,
\theta_{\varepsilon,i}\to 0
as
\varepsilon\to 0
,
\tau_{\varepsilon,i}
as in (
1.3
), and a function
u_{\varepsilon}\in E_{h_{\varepsilon},\beta_{\varepsilon}}
of the form

u_{\varepsilon}=w_{\varepsilon}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}(1+\theta_{\varepsilon,i})B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{\varepsilon,i},\tau_{\varepsilon,i}}+\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{\varepsilon},\tau_{\varepsilon}}+\Phi_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{\varepsilon},\theta_{\varepsilon},\tau_{\varepsilon}}\,,

(1.5)

where the approximate bubble
B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{\varepsilon,i},\tau_{\varepsilon,i}}\in H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
is as in Section
3.1
and the remainder
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)
-terms
\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{\varepsilon},\tau_{\varepsilon}}
and
\Phi_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{\varepsilon},\theta_{\varepsilon},\tau_{\varepsilon}}
are given by Propositions
3.2
and
4.2
. In particular,

\|\nabla\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{\varepsilon},\tau_{\varepsilon}}\|_{L^{2}}=\smallo(1),\quad\|\nabla\Phi_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{\varepsilon},\theta_{\varepsilon},\tau_{\varepsilon}}\|_{L^{2}}=\smallo(1),\quad\text{as }\varepsilon\to 0.

In contrast with several other works constructing blowing-up solutions to the Moser–Trudinger equation, starting with del Pino–Musso–Ruf
[
delPinoMusRuf1
,
delPinoMusRuf2
]
, our Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction will be performed in
H^{1}_{0}\left(\Omega\right)
, avoiding the use of weighted
C^{0}
-norms. While this is more in the spirit of the seminal work of Rey
[
Rey
]
, the elegance of working with the Hilbert space
H^{1}_{0}\left(\Omega\right)
requires a very precise ansatz (see Section
3
), and a very sharp analysis of the radial bubble
\overline{B}_{\gamma}
, as obtained in
[
DruThi
,
MalMar
,
ManMar
]
and further extended in our Section
6
. In fact, we will construct the ansatz in two steps. First we construct the approximate solution

U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}=w_{\varepsilon}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}+\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau},

for every
\tau
as in (
1.3
) and
\gamma
in a fairly broad range (see (
3.4
) and Proposition
3.2
). Then we shall strongly restrict the range of
\gamma
(Proposition
3.3
and (
3.28
)) and add the terms
\theta_{i}B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}
to the ansatz.
This will be crucial in the energy estimates of Section
4
. In order to estimate the error terms near the bubbles, we shall use the spherical profile of the bubble to treat the blow-up regions as approximate spheres and apply Poincaré–Sobolev-type estimates, as given in Section
7
.
Finally we will perform the Lyapunov-Schmidt reduction to find the correct value
(\gamma_{\varepsilon},\theta_{\varepsilon},\tau_{\varepsilon})
and the correction term
\Phi_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{\varepsilon},\theta_{\varepsilon},\tau_{\varepsilon}}
, to finally obtain
u_{\varepsilon}
as in (
1.5
) (see also Remark
4.6
).

Recently, Problem (

E

h
,
β

) has received attention also when the nonlinearity
f
is suitably perturbed. Mancini and the second author
[
ManThi
]
constructed radial (both positive and nodal) solutions
u_{\gamma}
to (

E

h
,
β

) on the unit disk, blowing up at
0
and having non-zero weak limit as
\gamma\to\infty
, in the case
h\equiv 1
,
f_{\gamma}(u)=\lambda_{\gamma}u+\beta_{\gamma}u\exp(u^{2})
or
f_{\gamma}(u)=\beta_{\gamma}u\exp(u^{2}-au)
for suitable
\lambda_{\gamma}
,
\beta_{\gamma}
and
a>0
. Grossi–Mancini–Naimen–Pistoia
[
GMNP
]
constructed nodal solutions
u_{p}
to (

E

h
,
β

) with
h\equiv 1
and
f_{p}(u)=u\exp\left(u^{2}+|u|^{p}\right)
, having
one
blow-up point as
p\downarrow 1
. Naimen
[
Nai
]
further gave a very detailed blow-up analysis of the blow-up of
radial
nodal solutions to (

E

h
,
β

) when
h\equiv 1
and
f(u)=u\exp\left(u^{2}+\alpha|u|^{\beta}\right)
,
\alpha>0
. To our knowledge, our work is the first one in which non-zero weak limits appear in the unperturbed case
f(u)=u\exp(u^{2})
, and an arbitrary number of bubbles concentrates at the same point. Indeed, these two phenomena cannot occur in the unperturbed case without (radial) symmetry breaking.

2.
Preliminary steps

This section is devoted to the construction of a smooth family of critical points satisfying some regularity, symmetry and asymptotic conditions which we will then use in the next sections to construct our blowing-up solutions.

Definition 2.1
.

For every
l\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
,
p\in\mathbb{N}
and
\alpha\in\left(0,1\right)
, we let
C_{l,\sym}^{p,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)
be the vector space of all functions in
C^{p,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)
that are even with respect to the line
\ell_{2j}
for all
j\in\left\{0,\dotsc,l-1\right\}
, where
\ell_{2j}
is as in (A).

Definition 2.2
.

Let
\beta>0
,
h
be a continuous, positive function on
\overline{\Omega}
and
w\in E_{h,\beta}
. Then we say that
w
is non-degenerate if there does not exist any solution
v\neq 0
to the problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta v=\lambda hf^{\prime}\left(w\right)v&&\text{in }\Omega\\
&v=0&&\text{on }\partial\Omega,\end{aligned}\right.

(2.1)

where
\lambda
and
f
are as in (
1.1
). We let
E_{h,\beta}^{nd}
be the set of all non-degenerate elements of
E_{h,\beta}
.

The main result of this section is the following:

Proposition 2.3
.

Let
\Omega
be a smooth, bounded domain,
l\in 2\mathbb{N}^{\star}
,
\beta_{0}\in\left(0,8l\pi\right)
,
\alpha\in\left(0,1\right)
and
h\in C^{l-2,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{0,\alpha}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
be a positive function such that
0\in\Omega
and
(A)
holds true. Then we have the following:

(i)

There exist
w_{0}\in E_{h,\beta_{0}}\cap C_{l,\sym}^{l,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{2}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
and
a_{0}>0
such that
w_{0}\left(x_{1},0\right)\sim a_{0}x_{1}^{l}
as
x_{1}\to 0
.

(ii)

There exists
\kappa_{0}>0
such that for every
\kappa\in\left(-\kappa_{0},\kappa_{0}\right)\backslash\left\{0\right\}
,
w_{\kappa}:=\left(1+\kappa\right)w_{0}\in E^{nd}_{h_{\kappa},\beta_{\kappa}}\cap C_{l,\sym}^{l,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{2}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
, where
\beta_{\kappa}:=(1+\kappa)^{2}\beta_{0}
and
h_{\kappa}:=h\exp\left(-\kappa\left(\kappa+2\right)w_{0}^{2}\right)
.

(iii)

For every
\kappa\in\left(-\kappa_{0},\kappa_{0}\right)\backslash\left\{0\right\}
, there exist
\hat{h}_{\kappa}\in C_{l,\sym}^{l-2,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{2}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
and
\varepsilon_{0}\left(\kappa\right)\in\left(0,1\right)
such that for every
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{0}\left(\kappa\right)\right)
, there exist
\beta_{\kappa,\varepsilon}>0
and
w_{\kappa,\varepsilon}\in E_{h_{\kappa,\varepsilon},\beta_{\kappa,\varepsilon}}\cap C_{l,\sym}^{l,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{2}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
, where
h_{\kappa,\varepsilon}:=h_{\kappa}+\varepsilon\hat{h}_{\kappa}
, such that the families
\left(\beta_{\kappa,\varepsilon}\right)_{0\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}\left(\kappa\right)}
and
\left(w_{\kappa,\varepsilon}\right)_{0\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}\left(\kappa\right)}
, where
\beta_{\kappa,0}:=\beta_{\kappa}
and
w_{\kappa,0}:=w_{\kappa}
, are smooth in
\varepsilon
and moreover
\partial_{\varepsilon}\left[w_{\kappa,\varepsilon}\left(0\right)\right]_{\varepsilon=0}<0
and
w_{\kappa,\varepsilon}\left(0\right)<0
for all
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{0}\left(\kappa\right)\right)
.

Proof of Proposition
2.3
(i).

Define

\Omega_{1}:=\left\{\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)\in\Omega:\,\left|x_{2}\right|<x_{1}\tan\left(\frac{\pi}{2l}\right)\right\}.

Since
\Omega
satisfies (A), we obtain that
\Omega_{1}
is symmetric with respect to the line
\ell_{0}
. In particular, we can define the vector space
\mathcal{H}
of all functions in
H^{1}_{0}\left(\Omega_{1}\right)
that are even in
x_{2}
. Note that (A) also gives that
\left.h\right|_{\Omega_{1}}
is even in
x_{2}
. By applying standard variational arguments (see for instance Proposition 6 of Mancini–Martinazzi
[
ManMar
]
in case
h\equiv 1
and
\mathcal{H}=H^{1}_{0}\left(\Omega_{1}\right)
), we then obtain that for every
\beta_{0}\in\left(0,8l\pi\right)
, there exists a critical point
w_{0}
of the functional
\left.I_{h}\right|_{\mathcal{H}}
under the constraint
\left\|\nabla w_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}\left(\Omega_{1}\right)}^{2}=\beta_{0}/2l
such that
w_{0}>0
in
\Omega_{1}
. By using (A), we can then extend
w_{0}
to the whole domain
\Omega
as an odd function with respect to the line
\ell_{2j+1}
for all
j\in\left\{0,\dotsc,l-1\right\}
. We claim that
w_{0}\in E_{h,\beta_{0}}
. To see this, for every test function
v\in H_{0}^{1}\left(\Omega\right)
, we define

v_{\sym}:=\sum_{j=0}^{l-1}v\circ S_{2j+1}\circ S_{1}-\sum_{j=0}^{l-1}v\circ S_{2j+1},

where
S_{2j+1}:\Omega\to\Omega
is the symmetry operator with respect to the line
\ell_{2j+1}
. By remarking that
v_{\sym}\in H_{0}^{1}\left(\Omega_{1}\right)
and using
v_{\sym}
as a test function for the Euler–Lagrange equation of
u_{0}
, we obtain

\int_{\Omega_{1}}\left<\nabla u_{0},\nabla v_{\sym}\right>dx=\frac{\beta_{0}}{l\int_{\Omega_{1}}hw_{0}^{2}\exp\left(w_{0}^{2}\right)dx}\int_{\Omega_{1}}hf\left(w_{0}\right)v_{\sym}dx.

(2.2)

By changes of variable and using the symmetry of
w_{0}
and
h
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{1}}\left<\nabla w_{0},\nabla v_{\sym}\right>dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\left<\nabla w_{0},\nabla v\right>dx,

(2.3)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{1}}hw_{0}^{2}\exp\left(w_{0}^{2}\right)dx

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2l}\int_{\Omega}hw_{0}^{2}\exp\left(w_{0}^{2}\right)dx=\frac{1}{2l}DI_{h}\left(w_{0}\right).w_{0},

(2.4)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{1}}hf\left(w_{0}\right)v_{\sym}dx

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}hf\left(w_{0}\right)vdx.

(2.5)

By putting together (
2.2
)–(
2.5
), we obtain

\int_{\Omega}\left<\nabla w_{0},\nabla v\right>dx=\frac{2\beta_{0}}{DI_{h}\left(w_{0}\right).w_{0}}\int_{\Omega}hf\left(w_{0}\right)vdx

and so
w_{0}\in E_{h,\beta_{0}}
. Since
h\in C^{l-2,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{0,\alpha}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
and
\partial\Omega
is smooth, by using the Moser–Trudinger inequality together with standard elliptic regularity theory, we then obtain that
w_{0}\in C^{l,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{2,\alpha}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
. Since
\left.w_{0}\right|_{\Omega_{1}}
is even in
x_{2}
and
w_{0}
is odd with respect to the line
\ell_{2j+1}
for all
j\in\left\{0,\dotsc,l-1\right\}
, we then obtain that
w_{0}
is even with respect to
\ell_{2j}
for all
j\in\left\{0,\dotsc,l-1\right\}
, i.e.
w_{0}\in C_{l,\sym}^{l,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)
. Furthermore, since
w_{0}\in C^{l,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)
and
w_{0}=0
on
\ell_{2j+1}
for all
j\in\left\{0,\dotsc,l-1\right\}
, we obtain that
D^{j}w_{0}\left(0\right)=0
for all
j\in\left\{0,\dotsc,l-1\right\}
and so

w_{0}\left(x_{1},0\right)=a_{0}x_{1}^{l}+\bigO\left(x_{1}^{l+\alpha}\right)

(2.6)

as
x_{1}\to 0
for some
a_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. It remains to prove that
a_{0}>0
. Since
0\in\Omega
and
\Omega
is open, there exists
r_{0}>0
such that
B\left(0,r_{0}\right)\subset\Omega
. For every
\varepsilon>0
, we define

S_{l,\varepsilon}\left(r_{0}\right):=\left\{\left(r\cos\theta,r\sin\theta\right):\,0<r<r_{0}\text{ and }\left|\theta\right|<\pi/\left(2\left(l+\varepsilon\right)\right)\right\}

and let
v_{l,\varepsilon}:S_{l,\varepsilon}\left(r_{0}\right)\to\mathbb{R}
be the function defined as

v_{l,\varepsilon}\left(r\cos\theta,r\sin\theta\right):=r^{l+\varepsilon}\cos\left(\left(l+\varepsilon\right)\theta\right)

for all
\left(r\cos\theta,r\sin\theta\right)\in S_{l,\varepsilon}\left(r_{0}\right)
. It is easy to check that
v_{l,\varepsilon}
is harmonic in
S_{l,\varepsilon}\left(r_{0}\right)
, continuous on
\overline{S_{l,\varepsilon}\left(r_{0}\right)}
and
v_{l,\varepsilon}=0
on
B\left(0,r_{0}\right)\cap\partial S_{l,\varepsilon}\left(r_{0}\right)
. On the other hand, since
S_{l,\varepsilon}\left(r_{0}\right)\subset\Omega_{1}
, we have that
w_{0}
is continuous on
\overline{S_{l,\varepsilon}\left(r_{0}\right)}
and positive on
\overline{S_{l,\varepsilon}\left(r_{0}\right)}\backslash\left\{0\right\}
. Furthermore, since
h,w_{0}>0
in
S_{l,\varepsilon}\left(r_{0}\right)
, it follows from the Euler–Lagrange equation of
w_{0}
that
\Delta w_{0}>0
in
S_{l,\varepsilon}\left(r_{0}\right)
. It follows that there exists
\delta_{l,\varepsilon}>0
such that
w_{0}\geq\delta_{l,\varepsilon}v_{l,\varepsilon}
on
\partial S_{l,\varepsilon}\left(r_{0}\right)\cap\partial B\left(0,r_{0}\right)
. By comparison, we then obtain that
w_{0}\geq\delta_{l,\varepsilon}v_{l,\varepsilon}
in
S_{l,\varepsilon}\left(r_{0}\right)
. Since
v_{l,\varepsilon}\left(r,0\right)=r^{l+\varepsilon}
, by taking
\varepsilon<\alpha
, we then obtain that the number
a_{0}
in (
2.6
) is positive. This ends the proof of (i) in Proposition
2.3
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.3
(ii).

It is easy to check that
w_{\kappa}\in E_{h_{\kappa},\beta_{\kappa}}\cap C_{l,\sym}^{2,\alpha}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
for all
\kappa\in\left(-1,1\right)
. It remains to prove that
w_{\kappa}\in E^{nd}_{h_{\kappa},\beta_{\kappa}}
for
\kappa\in\left(-\kappa_{0},\kappa_{0}\right)\backslash\left\{0\right\}
with
\kappa_{0}
small enough. Assume by contradiction that this is not the case, i.e. there exists a sequence of real numbers
\left(\kappa_{j}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
w_{\kappa_{j}}
is degenerate and
\kappa_{j}\to 0
. Let
v_{j}
be a nonzero solution of the linearized equation

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta v_{j}=\lambda_{\kappa_{j}}h_{\kappa_{j}}f^{\prime}\left(w_{\kappa_{j}}\right)v_{j}&&\text{in }\Omega\\
&v_{j}=0&&\text{on }\partial\Omega,\end{aligned}\right.

with

\lambda_{\kappa_{j}}:=\frac{2\beta_{\kappa_{j}}}{DI_{h_{\kappa_{j}}}\big(w_{\kappa_{j}}\big).w_{\kappa_{j}}}=\frac{2\beta_{0}}{DI_{h}\left(w_{0}\right).w_{0}}=\lambda.

By renormalizing and passing to a subsequences, we may assume without loss of generality that
\left\|\nabla v_{j}\right\|_{L^{2}}=1
and
\left(v_{j}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
converges weakly to some function
v_{0}
in
H^{1}_{0}\left(\Omega\right)
. By using the compactness of the embedding
H^{1}_{0}\left(\Omega\right)\hookrightarrow L^{2}\left(\Omega\right)
and remarking that
\beta_{\kappa}\to\beta_{0}
and
h_{\kappa},w_{\kappa}\to h,w_{0}
in
C^{0}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
, we obtain that
\left(v_{j}\right)_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
converges strongly to
v_{0}
in
H^{1}_{0}\left(\Omega\right)
and so
\left\|\nabla v_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}}=1
. Furthermore, we obtain that
v_{0}
is a solution of (
2.1
) with
\kappa=0
. By using the definitions of
h_{\kappa}
,
\beta_{\kappa}
and
w_{\kappa}
, in particular noticing that
h_{\kappa}\exp\left(w_{\kappa}^{2}\right)=h\exp\left(w_{0}^{2}\right)
, and recalling the equation satisfied by
v_{j}
and
v_{0}
, it follows that

\int_{\Omega}h\left(1+2w_{\kappa_{j}}^{2}\right)\exp\left(w_{0}^{2}\right)v_{j}v_{0}dx=\frac{1}{2}DI_{h}\left(w_{0}\right).w_{0}\int_{\Omega}\left<\nabla v_{j},\nabla v_{0}\right>dx=\int_{\Omega}hf^{\prime}\left(w_{0}\right)v_{j}v_{0}dx

and so

\int_{\Omega}hw_{0}^{2}\exp\left(w_{0}^{2}\right)v_{j}v_{0}dx=\int_{\Omega}h\frac{w_{\kappa_{j}}^{2}-w_{0}^{2}}{\kappa_{j}\left(\kappa_{j}+2\right)}\exp\left(w_{0}^{2}\right)v_{j}v_{0}dx=0.

(2.7)

By passing to the limit into (
2.7
), we obtain

\int_{\Omega}hw_{0}^{2}\exp\left(w_{0}^{2}\right)v_{0}^{2}dx=0,

which gives
w_{0}v_{0}=0
in
\Omega
. Since
\left\|\nabla w_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}}^{2}=\beta_{0}\neq 0
, by unique continuation (see Aronszajn
[
Aro
]
and Cordes
[
Cor
]
), we obtain that
w_{0}\neq 0
in a dense subset
D
of
\Omega
and so
v_{0}=0
on
D
. By continuity of
v_{0}
, it follows that
v_{0}=0
in
\Omega
. This is in contradiction with
\left\|\nabla v_{0}\right\|_{L^{2}}=1
. This ends the proof of (ii) in Proposition
2.3
.
∎

The result of (iii) in Proposition
2.3
will follow from the following:

Proposition 2.4
.

Let
l\in\mathbb{N}^{\star}
,
p\geq 2
,
\alpha\in\left(0,1\right)
and
\Omega
be a smooth, bounded domain such that
0\in\Omega
and
\Omega
is symmetric with respect to the line
\ell_{2j}
for all
j\in\left\{0,\dotsc,l-1\right\}
. Let
\overline{\beta}_{0}>0
,
\overline{h}\in C_{l,\sym}^{p-2,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{2}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
be positive in
\overline{\Omega}
and
\overline{w}_{0}\in E^{nd}_{\overline{h},\overline{\beta}_{0}}\cap C_{l,\sym}^{p,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{2}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
be such that
\overline{w}_{0}\left(0,0\right)=0
and
\overline{w}_{0}\left(r,0\right)>0
for small
r>0
. Let
D
be the set of all functions
\hat{h}\in C_{l,\sym}^{p-2,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{2}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
such that

\int_{\Omega}G_{\overline{h}}\left(\cdot,0\right)\hat{h}f\left(\overline{w}_{0}\right)dx<0,

where
G_{\overline{h}}
is the Green’s function of the operator

\Delta-\frac{2\overline{\beta}_{0}\overline{h}f^{\prime}\left(\overline{w}_{0}\right)}{DI_{\overline{h}}\left(\overline{w}_{0}\right).\overline{w}_{0}}

with boundary condition
\left.G_{\overline{h}}\left(\cdot,0\right)\right|_{\partial\Omega}=0
. Then
D
is a non-empty open subset of
C_{l,\sym}^{p-2,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{2}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
and for every
\hat{h}\in D
, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}>0
such that for every
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{0}\right)
, there exist
\overline{\beta}_{\varepsilon}>0
and
\overline{w}_{\varepsilon}\in E_{\overline{h}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\beta}_{\varepsilon}}\cap C_{l,\sym}^{p,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{2}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
, where
\overline{h}_{\varepsilon}:=\overline{h}+\varepsilon\hat{h}
, such that
\left(\overline{\beta}_{\varepsilon}\right)_{0\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}}
and
\left(\overline{w}_{\varepsilon}\right)_{0\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}}
are smooth in
\varepsilon
and
\partial_{\varepsilon}\left[\overline{w}_{\varepsilon}\left(0\right)\right]_{\varepsilon=0}<0
and
\overline{w}_{\varepsilon}\left(0\right)<0
for all
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{0}\right)
.

Proof.

We begin with proving that
D
is not empty. Since
G_{\overline{h}}\left(\cdot,0\right)>0
near 0,
\overline{w}_{0}\in C_{l,\sym}^{p,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)
and
\overline{w}_{0}\left(r,0\right)>0
for small
r>0
, we obtain that there exists
x_{0}\in\Omega
and
r_{0}>0
such that
G_{\overline{h}}\left(\cdot,0\right)\overline{w}_{0}>0
in
B\left(x_{0},r_{0}\right)
and
B\left(x_{0},r_{0}\right)\subset\Omega_{0}
, where

\Omega_{0}:=\left\{\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)\in\Omega:\,0<x_{2}<x_{1}\tan\left(\pi/l\right)\right\}.

Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}\left(\Omega\right)
be such that
\chi>0
in
B\left(x_{0},r_{0}\right)
and
\chi\equiv 0
in
B\left(x_{0},r_{0}\right)^{c}
. Let
\chi_{\sym}
be the unique function in
C_{l,\sym}^{p-2,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{2}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
such that
\chi_{\sym}\equiv\chi
in
\Omega_{0}
. By symmetry and since
G_{\overline{h}}\left(\cdot,0\right)\chi\overline{w}_{0}>0
in
B\left(x_{0},r_{0}\right)
and
\chi=0
in
B\left(x_{0},r_{0}\right)^{c}
, we obtain

\int_{\Omega}G_{\overline{h}}\left(\cdot,0\right)\chi_{\sym}f\left(\overline{w}_{0}\right)dx=2l\int_{\Omega}G_{\overline{h}}\left(\cdot,0\right)\chi f\left(\overline{w}_{0}\right)dx>0,

i.e.
-\chi_{\sym}\in D
. This proves that
D
is not empty. Now, we prove the second part of Proposition
2.4
. Since
\overline{h}\in C_{l,\sym}^{p-2,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{2}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
and
\overline{w}_{0}\in E^{nd}_{\overline{h},\overline{\beta}_{0}}\cap C_{l,\sym}^{p,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{2}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
, it follows from the implicit function theorem together with standard elliptic regularity that there exist a neighborhood
\mathcal{N}
of
\overline{h}
in
C_{l,\sym}^{p-2,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{2}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
and a smooth mapping
\overline{w}:\mathcal{N}\to C_{l,\sym}^{p,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{2}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
such that
\overline{w}\left(\overline{h}\right)=\overline{w}_{0}
and for every
\tilde{h}\in\mathcal{N}
,
\widetilde{U}=\overline{w}\big(\tilde{h}\big)
is a solution of the problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta\widetilde{U}=\frac{2\overline{\beta}_{0}\tilde{h}f\big(\widetilde{U}\big)}{DI_{\overline{h}}\left(\overline{w}_{0}\right).\overline{w}_{0}}&&\text{in }\Omega\\
&\widetilde{U}=0&&\text{on }\partial\Omega.\end{aligned}\right.

(2.8)

Note that (
2.8
) is equivalent to
\widetilde{U}\in E_{\tilde{h},\overline{\beta}\big(\tilde{h}\big)}
, where

\overline{\beta}\big(\tilde{h}\big):=\frac{\overline{\beta}_{0}DI_{\tilde{h}}\big(\widetilde{U}\big).\widetilde{U}}{DI_{\overline{h}}\left(\overline{w}_{0}\right).\overline{w}_{0}}\,.

In particular, we obtain that for every
\hat{h}\in D
, there exists
\varepsilon_{0}\in\left(0,1\right)
such that for every
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{0}\right)
, there exist
\overline{\beta}_{\varepsilon}=\overline{\beta}\left(\overline{h}_{\varepsilon}\right)>0
and
\overline{w}_{\varepsilon}=\overline{w}\left(\overline{h}_{\varepsilon}\right)\in E_{\overline{h}_{\varepsilon},\overline{\beta}_{\varepsilon}}\cap C_{l,\sym}^{p,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{2}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
, where
\overline{h}_{\varepsilon}:=\overline{h}+\varepsilon\hat{h}
such that
\left(\overline{\beta}_{\varepsilon}\right)_{0\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}}
and
\left(\overline{w}_{\varepsilon}\right)_{0\leq\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{0}}
are smooth in
\varepsilon
. Furthermore, by differentiating (
2.8
), we obtain

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\left(\Delta-2\overline{\beta}_{0}\left(DI_{\overline{h}}\left(\overline{w}_{0}\right).\overline{w}_{0}\right)^{-1}\overline{h}f^{\prime}\left(\overline{w}_{0}\right)\right)\partial_{\varepsilon}\left[\overline{w}_{\varepsilon}\right]_{\varepsilon=0}=\frac{2\overline{\beta}_{0}\hat{h}f\left(\overline{w}_{0}\right)}{DI_{\overline{h}}\left(\overline{w}_{0}\right).\overline{w}_{0}}&&\text{in }\Omega\\
&\partial_{\varepsilon}\left[\overline{w}_{\varepsilon}\right]_{\varepsilon=0}=0&&\text{on }\partial\Omega.\end{aligned}\right.

Since
\hat{h}\in D
, it follows that

\partial_{\varepsilon}\left[\overline{w}_{\varepsilon}\left(0\right)\right]_{\varepsilon=0}=\int_{\Omega}G_{\overline{h}}\left(\cdot,0\right)\hat{h}f\left(\overline{w}_{0}\right)dx<0.

Since
\overline{w}_{0}\left(0\right)=0
, by taking
\varepsilon_{0}
smaller if necessary, we then obtain that
\overline{w}_{\varepsilon}\left(0\right)<0
for all
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{0}\right)
. This ends the proof of Proposition
2.4
.
∎

Proof of Proposition
2.3
(iii).

The result of (iii) in Proposition
2.3
is a direct consequence of Proposition
2.4
applied to
\overline{\beta}_{0}:=\beta_{\kappa}
,
\overline{h}_{0}:=h_{\kappa}
and
\overline{w}_{0}:=w_{\kappa}
.
∎

3.
Construction of the ansatz

This section is devoted to the construction of our ansatz. We let
\Omega
,
l
,
\alpha
and
h
be as in Theorem
1.2
, fix
\beta>4\pi
,
\beta_{0}>0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that
\beta=\beta_{0}+4k\pi
and let
a_{0}
,
\kappa_{0}
,
\varepsilon_{0}
,
\beta_{\kappa}
,
w_{\kappa}
,
h_{\kappa}
,
\beta_{\kappa,\varepsilon}
,
u_{\kappa,\varepsilon}
and
h_{\kappa,\varepsilon}
be as in Proposition
2.3
. To prove that
\beta
is an unstable energy level of
I_{h}
, by using a diagonal argument, one can easily see that it suffices to show that for every
\kappa\in\left(-\kappa_{0},\kappa_{0}\right)\backslash\left\{0\right\}
, the number
\beta_{\kappa}+4k\pi
is an unstable energy level of
I_{h_{\kappa}}
. In what follows, we fix
\kappa\in\left(-\kappa_{0},\kappa_{0}\right)\backslash\left\{0\right\}
and for the sake of simplicity, we drop the dependance in
\kappa
from our notations. More precisely, we denote
\varepsilon_{0}:=\varepsilon_{0}\left(\kappa\right)
,
\beta_{0}:=\beta_{\kappa}
,
h_{0}:=h_{\kappa}
,
w_{0}:=w_{\kappa}
,
\beta_{\varepsilon}:=\beta_{\kappa,\varepsilon}
,
h_{\varepsilon}:=h_{\kappa,\varepsilon}
and
w_{\varepsilon}:=w_{\kappa,\varepsilon}
. Remark that the new function
w_{0}
still satisfies the properties of (i) in Proposition
2.3
but now this function is moreover non-degenerate.

3.1.
The bubbles

For every
\gamma_{0}>0
, we let
\overline{B}_{\gamma_{0}}
be the unique radial solution to the problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta\overline{B}_{\gamma_{0}}=f\left(\overline{B}_{\gamma_{0}}\right)&&\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\\
&\overline{B}_{\gamma_{0}}\left(0\right)=\gamma_{0},&&\end{aligned}\right.

where
f\left(s\right):=s\exp\left(s^{2}\right)
for all
s\in\mathbb{R}
. Note that by standard ordinary differential equations theory,
\overline{B}_{\gamma_{0}}
is defined on
\left[0,\infty\right)
. For every
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{0}\right)
,
\gamma_{0}>0
and
x_{0}\in\Omega
, we then define

\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{0},x_{0}}\left(x\right):=\overline{B}_{\gamma_{0}}\big(\sqrt{\lambda_{\varepsilon}h_{\varepsilon}\left(x_{0}\right)}\left|x-x_{0}\right|\big)\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{2},

where

\lambda_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{2\beta_{\varepsilon}}{DI_{h_{\varepsilon}}\left(w_{\varepsilon}\right).w_{\varepsilon}}\longrightarrow\frac{2\beta}{DI_{h}\left(w_{0}\right).w_{0}}=:\lambda>0,

so that
\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{0},x_{0}}
solves the problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{0},x_{0}}=\lambda_{\varepsilon}h_{\varepsilon}\left(x_{0}\right)f\left(\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{0},x_{0}}\right)&&\text{in }\mathbb{R}^{2}\\
&\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{0},x_{0}}\left(x_{0}\right)=\gamma_{0}.&&\end{aligned}\right.

For every
r>0
such that
B\left(x_{0},r\right)\subset\Omega
, we then let
B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{0},x_{0},r}:\Omega\to\mathbb{R}
be the function defined as

B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{0},x_{0},r}\left(x\right):=\left\{\begin{aligned} &\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{0},x_{0}}\left(x\right)-C_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{0},x_{0},r}+A_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{0},x_{0},r}H\left(x,x_{0}\right)&&x\in B\left(x_{0},r\right)\\
&A_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{0},x_{0},r}G\left(x,x_{0}\right)&&\text{otherwise}\end{aligned}\right.

(3.1)

for all
x\in\Omega
, where
G
is the Green’s function of the Laplace operator in
\Omega
with boundary condition
\left.G\left(\cdot,x_{0}\right)\right|_{\partial\Omega}=0
,
H
is the regular part of
G
, i.e.

G\left(x,x_{0}\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln\frac{1}{\left|x-x_{0}\right|}+H\left(x,x_{0}\right)

and
A_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{0},x_{0},r}
,
C_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{0},x_{0},r}
are constants chosen so that
B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{0},x_{0},r}\in C^{1}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
, i.e.

\displaystyle A_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{0},x_{0},r}

\displaystyle:=\int_{B(x_{0},r)}\Delta\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{0},x_{0}}\,dx,

(3.2)

\displaystyle C_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{0},x_{0},r}

\displaystyle:=\overline{B}_{\gamma_{0}}\big(\sqrt{\lambda_{\varepsilon}h_{\varepsilon}\left(x_{0}\right)}r\big)-\frac{A_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{0},x_{0},r}}{2\pi}\ln\frac{1}{r}\,.

(3.3)

3.2.
The primary ansatz

For every
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{0}\right)
and
\delta\in\left(0,1\right)
, let
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{k}
and
T_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)
be the sets of parameters defined as

\displaystyle\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)

\displaystyle:=\big\{\gamma=\left(\gamma_{1},\dotsc,\gamma_{k}\right)\in\left(0,\infty\right)^{k}:\,\left|\gamma_{i}-\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\right|<\delta\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon},\,\forall i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}\big\},

(3.4)

\displaystyle T_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)

\displaystyle:=\Big\{\tau=\left(\tau_{1},\dotsc,\tau_{k}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:\,{-\frac{kd_{\varepsilon}}{\delta}<\tau_{1}<\dots<\tau_{k}<\frac{kd_{\varepsilon}}{\delta}}

\displaystyle\hskip 139.0pt\text{and }\left|\tau_{i}-\tau_{j}\right|>\delta d_{\varepsilon},\,\forall i,j\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\},i\neq j\Big\},

(3.5)

where

\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}:=\frac{2\left(k+l-1\right)}{l\left|w_{\varepsilon}\left(0\right)\right|}\ln\frac{1}{\left|w_{\varepsilon}\left(0\right)\right|}\quad\text{and}\quad d_{\varepsilon}:=\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{-1/l}.

(3.6)

From (
3.6
),
w_{0}(0)=0
and
\partial_{\varepsilon}[w_{\varepsilon}(0)]_{\varepsilon=0}\neq 0
, we get

w_{\varepsilon}(0)\sim-\frac{2\left(k+l-1\right)}{l}\frac{\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}},\quad\varepsilon\sim\frac{w_{\varepsilon}(0)}{\partial_{\varepsilon}[w_{\varepsilon}(0)]_{\varepsilon=0}}=\bigO\left(\frac{\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}\right),\quad\text{as }\varepsilon\to 0,

(3.7)

and since
w_{0}\left(r,0\right)\sim a_{0}r^{l}
as
r\to 0
, using the continuity of
\partial_{\varepsilon}w_{\varepsilon}(x)
jointly in
\varepsilon
and
x
, and (
3.7
) we get for some
\varepsilon_{1}\in(0,\varepsilon)

\begin{split}w_{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)&=w_{0}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)+[w_{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)-w_{0}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)]=\bigO\left(d_{\varepsilon}^{l}\right)+\varepsilon\partial_{\varepsilon}[w_{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)]_{\varepsilon=\varepsilon_{1}}\\
&\sim\varepsilon\partial_{\varepsilon}[w_{\varepsilon}\left(0\right)]_{\varepsilon=0}\sim w_{\varepsilon}\left(0\right)\sim-\frac{2\left(k+l-1\right)\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{l\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}},\quad\text{as }\varepsilon\to 0,\end{split}

(3.8)

uniformly in
\tau\in T_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)
.
For every
(\gamma,\tau)\in\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)\times T_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)
, we define

\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}:=w_{\varepsilon}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}},

where
B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}:=B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}}
,
\overline{\tau_{i}}:=\left(\tau_{i},0\right)
, and for
\delta_{0}\in(0,1/2)
to be fixed later,

r_{\varepsilon}:=\overline{\mu}_{\varepsilon}^{\delta_{0}},\quad\overline{\mu}_{\varepsilon}^{2}:=\exp\big(-\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}\big).

(3.9)

Claim 3.1
.

Set
A_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}:=A_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}}
and
C_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}:=C_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}}
.
For every
\delta\in\left(0,1\right)
and
i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}
, we have

\displaystyle A_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}

\displaystyle=\frac{4\pi}{\gamma_{i}}+\bigO\left(\frac{1}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{3}}\right)\,,\quad C_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}=-\frac{2\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{\gamma_{i}}+\bigO\left(\frac{1}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}\right),

(3.10)

\displaystyle\partial_{\gamma_{i}}\left[A_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{4\pi}{\gamma_{i}^{2}}+\bigO\left(\frac{1}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{4}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{\gamma_{i}}\left[C_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right]=\frac{2\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{\gamma_{i}^{2}}+\bigO\left(\frac{1}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right)

(3.11)

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, uniformly in
\left(\gamma,\tau\right)\in\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)\times T_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)
. Furthermore, for every
a\geq 0
and
\delta^{\prime}\in\left(0,1-\sqrt{\delta_{0}}\right)
(i.e. such that
\left(1-\delta^{\prime}\right)^{2}>\delta_{0}
), we have

\partial_{\tau_{i}}\left[A_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right]=\bigO\left(\frac{1}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{a}}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\partial_{\tau_{i}}\left[C_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right]\sim-\frac{\partial_{x_{1}}h_{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)}{h_{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)\gamma_{i}}=\bigO\left(\frac{1}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}\right)

(3.12)

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, uniformly in
\left(\gamma,\tau\right)\in\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta^{\prime}\right)\times T_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)
.

The proof of Claim
3.1
is based on a precise asymptotic study of the bubbles
\overline{B}_{\gamma}
and is postponed to the Appendix.

3.3.
Correction of the error at the bottom of the bubbles

In this section, we modify our ansatz so to correct the error made outside the balls
B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},2r_{\varepsilon}\right)
. We prove the following:

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
\Omega
,
l

\alpha
and
h
be as in Theorem
1.2
. Let
k
,
\varepsilon_{0}
,
h_{\varepsilon}
,
w_{\varepsilon}
,
\lambda_{\varepsilon}
,
\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}
,
\overline{\tau_{i}}
,
r_{\varepsilon}
,
\delta_{0}
,
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)
,
T_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)
and
\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}
be as in Sections
3.1
and
3.2
. Let
\chi\in C^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}\right)
be such that
0\leq\chi\leq 1
in
\mathbb{R}
,
\chi\equiv 1
in
\left(-\infty,1\right]
and
\chi\equiv 0
in
\left[2,\infty\right)
. Define

\chi_{\varepsilon,\tau}\left(x\right):=1-\sum_{i=1}^{k}\chi\left(\left(\left|x-\overline{\tau_{i}}\right|+r_{\varepsilon}^{2}-r_{\varepsilon}\right)/r_{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)\qquad\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}.

For every
\delta\in\left(0,1\right)
and
\delta^{\prime}\in(0,1-\sqrt{2\delta_{0}})
, there exist
\varepsilon_{1}(\delta,\delta^{\prime})\in(0,\varepsilon_{0})

and
C_{1}=C_{1}\left(\delta,\delta^{\prime}\right)>0
such that for every
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{1}\left(\delta,\delta^{\prime}\right)\right)
and
(\gamma,\tau)\in\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta^{\prime}\right)\times T_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)
, there exists a unique solution
\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\in C^{l,\alpha}\left(\Omega\right)\cap C^{2}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
to the problem

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\Delta\left(w_{\varepsilon}+\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\right)=\lambda_{\varepsilon}h_{\varepsilon}\chi_{\varepsilon,\tau}f\big(\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}+\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\big)&&\text{in }\Omega\\
&\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}=0&&\text{on }\partial\Omega\end{aligned}\right.

(3.13)

such that
\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}
is even in
x_{2}
, continuously differentiable in
(\gamma,\tau)
and

\displaystyle\left\|\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\right\|_{C^{1}}\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}\,,\quad\left\|D_{\gamma}\left[\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\right]\right\|_{C^{1}}\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}},

(3.14)

\displaystyle\left\|D_{\tau}\left[\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\right]\right\|_{H^{1}}+\left\|D_{\tau}\left[\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\right]\right\|_{C^{0}}\leq\frac{C_{1}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}\,.

(3.15)

Finally, setting
U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}:=\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}+\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}
, there exists
p_{0}=p_{0}\left(\delta_{0},\delta^{\prime}\right)
such that for every
p\in\left[1,p_{0}\right]
,
a\geq 0
and
i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\exp\big(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}^{2}\big)\mathbf{1}_{A\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon},R_{\varepsilon}\right)}\right\|_{L^{p}}=\bigO\left(\frac{1}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{a}}\right),\,\left\|\exp\big(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}^{2}\big)B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}^{a}\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{R_{\varepsilon},\tau}}\right\|_{L^{p}}=\bigO\left(\frac{1}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{a}}\right),

(3.16)

\displaystyle\left\|\partial_{\tau_{i}}\left[\chi_{\varepsilon,\tau}\right]f\left(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\right)\right\|_{L^{p}}=\bigO\left(\frac{1}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{a}}\right),\,\left\|f^{\prime}\left(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\right)\partial_{\tau_{i}}\left[U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\right]\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{r_{\varepsilon},\tau}}\right\|_{L^{p}}=\bigO\left(\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\right)

(3.17)

uniformly in
(\gamma,\tau)\in\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta^{\prime}\right)\times T_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)
, where
R_{\varepsilon}:=\exp\left(-\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\right)
and

A\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r,R\right):=B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},R\right)\backslash B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\Omega_{r,\tau}:=\Omega\backslash\left(\bigcup_{i=1}^{k}B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r\right)\right)

(3.18)

for all
R>r>0

In other words, the function

U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}:=\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}+\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}=w_{\varepsilon}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}+\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau},

(3.19)

where
\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}
is given by Proposition
3.2
, is an exact solution outside the balls
B\left(\overline{\tau}_{i},r_{\varepsilon}+r_{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)
for all
i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}
, and it satisfies

\Delta U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\Delta B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}=\Delta\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\overline{\tau_{i}}}=\lambda_{\varepsilon}h_{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)f\big(\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\overline{\tau_{i}}}\big)&\text{in }B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}\right)\\
\lambda_{\varepsilon}h_{\varepsilon}\chi_{\varepsilon,\tau}f\left(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\right)&\text{in }\Omega_{r_{\varepsilon},\tau}.\end{array}\right.

(3.20)

Since the proof of Proposition
3.2
is lenghty, but not necessary to understand the rest of the construction, it is postponed to Section
5
.

For later use, we also observe that (
3.1
), (
3.6
), (
3.9
), (
3.10
) and (
3.14
) give
U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}=\delta_{0}\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}(1+\smallo(1))
in
\Omega^{i}_{\varepsilon}:=B(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}+r_{\varepsilon}^{2})\setminus B(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon})
, hence

f(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau})=\bigO\Big(\overline{\mu}_{\varepsilon}^{-2\delta_{0}^{2}+\smallo(1)}\Big),\quad\text{in }\Omega^{i}_{\varepsilon}.

(3.21)

3.4.
Adjustment of the values at the centers of the bubbles

In this section, we refine the range of the parameters
\gamma_{i}
so to optimize the error made in the regions
B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}\right)
. Let us start by expanding

U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\left(x\right)=\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\overline{\tau_{i}}}\left(x\right)+E^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}+F^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\left(x\right)

(3.22)

for all
x\in B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}\right)
,
where

\displaystyle E^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}:=w_{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)-C_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}+A_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}H\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)+\sum_{j\neq i}A_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{j},\tau_{j}}G\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},\overline{\tau_{j}}\right)+\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right),

(3.23)

\displaystyle F^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\left(x\right):=w_{\varepsilon}\left(x\right)-w_{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)+A_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\left(H(x,\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)-H\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},\overline{\tau_{i}})\right)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad+\sum_{j\neq i}A_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{j},\tau_{j}}\left(G\left(x,\overline{\tau_{j}}\right)-G\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},\overline{\tau_{j}}\right)\right)+\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\left(x\right)-\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right).

(3.24)

Note that
F^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)=0
, so
F^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}
is small in
B\left(\overline{\tau}_{i},r_{\varepsilon}\right)
. Instead the constant
E^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}
might be large depending on the choice of
\gamma
and
\tau
. In the next proposition we show that we can choose
\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)\sim\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}
depending on
\tau
and
\varepsilon
in such a way that
E^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right),\tau}=0
for all
i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}
.

Proposition 3.3
.

Let
\delta_{0}
,
\varepsilon_{1}
and
\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}
be as in Proposition
3.2
. Then for every
\delta\in\left(0,1\right)
and
\delta^{\prime}\in\left(0,1-\sqrt{2\delta_{0}}\right)
, there exists
\varepsilon_{2}\left(\delta,\delta^{\prime}\right)\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{1}\left(\delta,\delta^{\prime}\right)\right)
such that for every
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{2}\left(\delta,\delta^{\prime}\right)\right)
and
\tau\in T_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)
, there exists a unique
\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)=\left(\overline{\gamma}_{1,\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right),\dotsc,\overline{\gamma}_{k,\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)\right)\in\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta^{\prime}\right)
such that
\overline{\gamma}_{k,\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)
is continuous in
\tau
and for every
i\in\left\{1,\dots,k\right\}
, we have

U_{\varepsilon,\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right),\tau}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)=\overline{\gamma}_{i,\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)\quad\text{and}\quad\overline{\gamma}_{i,\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)\sim\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}

(3.25)

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, uniformly in
\tau\in T_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)
.

Proof.

For every
\gamma\in\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta^{\prime}\right)
, we denote
\tilde{\gamma}:=\gamma/\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}
. We let
I:=\left(1-\delta^{\prime},1+\delta^{\prime}\right)
and
E_{\varepsilon,\tau}:I^{k}\to\mathbb{R}^{k}
,
E_{\varepsilon,\tau}=\big(E_{\varepsilon,\tau}^{\left(1\right)},\dotsc,E_{\varepsilon,\tau}^{(k)}\big)
be the function defined by

E_{\varepsilon,\tau}^{\left(i\right)}\left(\tilde{\gamma}\right):=\frac{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}E^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\quad\forall\gamma\in I^{k},\,i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}.

In particular,
E_{\varepsilon,\tau}\in C^{1}(I^{k})
. By definition of
d_{\varepsilon}
,
G
and
H
, we obtain

G\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},\overline{\tau_{j}}\right)\sim\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln\frac{1}{d_{\varepsilon}}\sim\frac{\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{2l\pi}\quad\text{and}\quad H\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)=\bigO\left(1\right)

(3.26)

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, uniformly in
\tau\in T_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)
. It follows from (
3.8
), (
3.10
), (
3.11
), (
3.14
) and (
3.26
) that
E_{\varepsilon,\tau}\to E_{0}=\big(E_{0}^{\left(1\right)},\cdots,E_{0}^{(k)}\big)
in
C^{1}(I^{k})
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, uniformly in
\tau\in T^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\delta\right)
, where

E_{0}^{\left(i\right)}\left(\tilde{\gamma}\right):=\frac{2}{\tilde{\gamma}_{i}}+\frac{2}{l}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{\tilde{\gamma}_{j}}-\frac{2\left(k+l-1\right)}{l}

for all
i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}
and
\tilde{\gamma}=\left(\tilde{\gamma}_{1},\dotsc,\tilde{\gamma}_{k}\right)\in I^{k}
. In particular,

E_{0}\left(1,\dotsc,1\right)=0\quad\text{and}\quad\det\left(DE_{0}\left(1,\dotsc,1\right)\right)\neq 0.

(3.27)

By applying the implicit function theorem, it follows from (
3.27
) that there exists
\varepsilon_{2}\left(\delta,\delta^{\prime}\right)\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{1}\left(\delta,\delta^{\prime}\right)\right)
such that for every
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{2}\left(\delta,\delta^{\prime}\right)\right)
and
\tau\in T_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)
, there exists a unique
\tilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)\in I^{k}
such that
\tilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)
is continuous in
\tau
,
E_{\varepsilon,\tau}\left(\tilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)\right)=0
and
\tilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)\to\left(1,\dotsc,1\right)
as
\varepsilon\to 0
, uniformly in
\tau\in T_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)
, i.e. there exists a unique
\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)=\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\tilde{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)\in\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta^{\prime}\right)
such that
\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)
is continuous in
\tau
and (
3.25
) holds true. This ends the proof of Proposition
3.3
.
∎

Now, we refine the set
\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{k}(\delta^{\prime})
by defining

\overline{\Gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\tau\right):=\big\{\gamma=\left(\gamma_{1},\dotsc,\gamma_{k}\right)\in\left(0,\infty\right)^{k}:\,|\gamma_{i}-\overline{\gamma}_{i,\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)|<\frac{\delta_{\varepsilon}}{{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}},\;\forall i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}\big\},

(3.28)

where
\overline{\gamma}_{1,\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right),\dotsc,\overline{\gamma}_{k,\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)
are the numbers obtained in Proposition
3.3
and

\delta_{\varepsilon}:=\overline{\mu}_{\varepsilon}^{\delta_{1}+1/2},

(3.29)

where
\overline{\mu}_{\varepsilon}
is as in (
3.9
) and
\delta_{1}\in\left(0,1/2\right)
is a number that we shall fix later.

Note that for every
\delta,\delta^{\prime}\in\left(0,1\right)
, we have

\overline{\Gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\tau\right)\subset\Gamma_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta^{\prime}\right)

(3.30)

for small
\varepsilon>0
, uniformly in
\tau\in T_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)
. Therefore, we can fix

\delta^{\prime}:=\frac{1-\sqrt{2\delta_{0}}}{2}

in what follows and let
\varepsilon_{3}\left(\delta\right)\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{2}\left(\delta,\delta^{\prime}\right)\right)
be such that (
3.30
) holds true together with the results of Propositions
3.2
and
3.3
for all
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{3}\left(\delta\right)\right)
and
\tau\in T_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)
.

3.5.
An additional variation in the directions of the bubbles

We now introduce an additional family of parameters
\theta=\left(\theta_{1},\dotsc,\theta_{k}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
and define our final ansatz as

U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}:=U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\theta_{i}B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}=w_{\varepsilon}+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(1+\theta_{i}\right)B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}+\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau},

for

\theta\in\Theta^{k}_{\varepsilon}(\delta):=\bigg\{\theta=\left(\theta_{1},\dotsc,\theta_{k}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{k}:\,\left|\theta_{i}\right|<\frac{\delta_{\varepsilon}\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{4}},\;\forall i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}\bigg\},

(3.31)

where
\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}
and
\delta_{\varepsilon}
are as in (
3.6
) and (
3.29
). Finally, we define

P^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\delta\right):=\Big\{\left(\gamma,\tau,\theta\right)\in\left(0,\infty\right)^{k}\times T^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\delta\right)\times\Theta^{k}_{\varepsilon}:\,\gamma\in\overline{\Gamma}^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)\Big\},

where
T^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\delta\right)
,
\overline{\Gamma}^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)
and
\Theta^{k}_{\varepsilon}
are defined as in (
3.5
), (
3.28
) and (
3.31
), respectively.

3.6.
Pointwise estimates near the centers of the bubbles

We can now prove the following:

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)
be as in Proposition
3.3
. Then for every
i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}
and
\delta\in\left(0,1\right)
we have

\displaystyle\partial_{\gamma_{i}}\big[E^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\big]=-\frac{2\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}+\smallo\left(\frac{\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right),\quad\partial_{\gamma_{j}}\big[E^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\big]=-\frac{2\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{l\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}+\smallo\left(\frac{\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right),\quad\text{for }j\neq i,

\displaystyle E^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}=-\frac{2\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\left((\gamma_{i}-\overline{\gamma}_{i,\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right))+\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{\gamma_{j}-\overline{\gamma}_{j,\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)}{l}\right)+\smallo\left(\left|\gamma-\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)\right|\frac{\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right)

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, uniformly in
\tau\in T_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right)
and
\gamma\in\overline{\Gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\tau\right)
.

Proof.

Using (
3.11
), (
3.23
), (
3.14
) and noticing that for
\left(\gamma_{1},\dots,\gamma_{k}\right)\in\overline{\Gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\tau\right)
we have
\gamma_{j}\sim\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}
for
j=1,\dots,k
, we get

\displaystyle\partial_{\gamma_{i}}\big[E^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\big]

\displaystyle=-\partial_{\gamma_{i}}\left[C_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right]+\partial_{\gamma_{i}}\left[A_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right]H\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)+\partial_{\gamma_{i}}\left[\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{2\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{\gamma_{i}^{2}}\left(1+\smallo\left(1\right)\right)-\frac{4\pi}{\gamma_{i}^{2}}\bigO\left(1\right)+\bigO\left(\frac{1}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right)=-\frac{2\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}+\smallo\left(\frac{\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right)

as
\varepsilon\to 0
. For the case
j\neq i
, using (
3.6
), we estimate

G\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},\overline{\tau_{j}}\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi}\ln\frac{1}{d_{\varepsilon}}+\bigO\left(1\right)=\frac{1}{2\pi l}\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}+\bigO\left(1\right),

uniformly in
\tau\in T_{\varepsilon}^{k}\left(\delta\right),

hence

\displaystyle\partial_{\gamma_{j}}\big[E^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\big]

\displaystyle=\partial_{\gamma_{j}}\left[A_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{j},\tau_{j}}\right]G\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},\overline{\tau_{j}}\right)+\partial_{\gamma_{j}}\left[\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\right]

\displaystyle=-\frac{4\pi}{\gamma_{j}^{2}}\left(\frac{1}{2\pi l}\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}+\bigO\left(1\right)\right)+\bigO\left(\frac{1}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right)=-\frac{2\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{l\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}+\smallo\left(\frac{\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right).

Now, since
E^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right),\tau}=0
, integrating the gradient of
E^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}
with respect to
\gamma
from
\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)
to a generic
\gamma\in\overline{\Gamma}^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)
, the last identity follows at once.
∎

Proposition 3.5
.

For every
i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}
and
\delta\in\left(0,1\right)
, we have

F^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\left(x\right)=\Bigg(a_{0}l\tau_{i}^{l-1}-\frac{2}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{\tau_{i}-\tau_{j}}\Bigg)\left(x_{1}-\tau_{i}\right)+\smallo\left(\frac{\left|x-\overline{\tau_{i}}\right|}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}d_{\varepsilon}}\right),

(3.32)

and for every
i,j\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}
,

\partial_{\gamma_{j}}\big[F^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\big]\left(x\right)=\bigO\left(\frac{\left|x-\overline{\tau_{i}}\right|}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}d_{\varepsilon}}\right)

(3.33)

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, uniformly in
x=\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)\in B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}\right)
and
\left(\gamma,\tau,\theta\right)\in P^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\delta\right)
.

Proof.

Note that
F^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)=0
. Then, by using (
3.7
), (
3.10
) and (
3.15
) and since
w_{\varepsilon}=w_{0}+\bigO\left(\varepsilon\right)
in
C^{1}\left(\Omega\right)
,
w_{0}\left(r,0\right)\sim a_{0}r^{l}
as
r\to 0
and
\partial_{x_{2}}w_{0}\left(0,0\right)=0
, we obtain

F^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\left(x\right)=\int_{0}^{1}\left<\nabla F^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\left(\left(1-t\right)\overline{\tau_{i}}+tx\right),x-\overline{\tau_{i}}\right>dt\\
=\int_{0}^{1}\Bigg(\left<\nabla w_{0}\left(\left(1-t\right)\overline{\tau_{i}}+tx\right),x-\overline{\tau_{i}}\right>-\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{A_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{j},\tau_{j}}\left<\left(1-t\right)\overline{\tau_{i}}+tx-\overline{\tau_{j}},x-\overline{\tau_{i}}\right>}{2\pi\left|\left(1-t\right)\overline{\tau_{i}}+tx-\overline{\tau_{j}}\right|^{2}}\Bigg)dt\\
+\bigO\left(\varepsilon\left|x-\overline{\tau}_{i}\right|\right)=\Bigg(a_{0}l\tau_{i}^{l-1}-\frac{2}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{\tau_{i}-\tau_{j}}\Bigg)\left(x_{1}-\tau_{i}\right)+\smallo\left(\frac{\left|x-\overline{\tau}_{i}\right|}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{1-1/l}}\right)

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, uniformly in
x=\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)\in B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}\right)
,
\tau\in T^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\delta\right)
and
\gamma\in\overline{\Gamma}^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)
, hence proving (
3.32
). Differentiating (
3.24
) and using Claim
3.1
, (
3.33
) also follows at once.
∎

Proposition 3.6
.

For every
i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}
and
\delta\in\left(0,1\right)
, we have

U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}\left(x\right)=\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\left(x\right)+\Bigg(a_{0}l\tau_{i}^{l-1}-\frac{2}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}\sum_{j\neq i}\frac{1}{\tau_{i}-\tau_{j}}\Bigg)\left(x_{1}-\tau_{i}\right)\\
+\smallo\left(\frac{\left|x-\overline{\tau_{i}}\right|}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{1-1/l}}\right)+\bigO\left(\frac{\delta_{\varepsilon}\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{3}}\right)

(3.34)

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, uniformly in
x=\left(x_{1},x_{2}\right)\in B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}\right)
and
\left(\gamma,\tau,\theta\right)\in P^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\delta\right)
. In particular, for every
\delta\in\left(0,1\right)
, there exists
\varepsilon_{4}\left(\delta\right)\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{3}\left(\delta\right)\right)
, where
\varepsilon_{3}\left(\delta\right)
is as in Section
3.5
, such that

\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\left(x\right)>0\text{ and }U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}>0\text{ in }B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}\right)

(3.35)

for all
\varepsilon\left(\delta\right)\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{4}\left(\delta\right)\right)
,
\left(\gamma,\tau,\theta\right)\in P^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\delta\right)
and
i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}
.

Proof.

In order to prove (
3.34
), it suffices to write

U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}\left(x\right)=\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\left(x\right)+E^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}+F^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\left(x\right)+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\theta_{j}B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{j},\tau_{j}}\left(x\right)\quad\text{in }B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}\right)

and apply Proposition
3.4
to bound

E^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}+\sum_{j=1}^{k}\theta_{i}B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{j},\tau_{j}}=\bigO\left(\frac{\delta_{\varepsilon}\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{3}}\right)

and Proposition
3.5
to estimate
F^{\left(i\right)}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\left(x\right)
. It then follows from (
3.34
) and (
6.2
) that (
3.35
) holds true for small
\varepsilon>0
, uniformly in
\left(\gamma,\tau,\theta\right)\in P^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\delta\right)
.
∎

4.
Proof of Theorems
1.2
and
1.3

This section is devoted to the proof of Theorems
1.2
and
1.3
. We let
\Omega
,
l
,
\alpha
and
h
be as in Theorem
1.2
, fix
\beta>4\pi
,
\beta_{0}>0
and
k\in\mathbb{N}^{*}
such that
\beta=\beta_{0}+4k\pi
and let
\beta_{\varepsilon}
,
h_{\varepsilon}
,
w_{\varepsilon}
,
\lambda_{\varepsilon}
,
\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}
,
\overline{\mu}_{\varepsilon}
,
d_{\varepsilon}
,
r_{\varepsilon}
,
\delta_{\varepsilon}
,
\delta_{0}
,
\delta_{1}
,
\overline{\gamma}_{i,\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)
,
\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{0},x_{0}}
,
A_{\varepsilon,\gamma,x,r}
,
C_{\varepsilon,\gamma,x,r}
,
G
,
H
,
B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}
,
\widetilde{U}_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}
,
\chi_{\varepsilon,\tau}
,
\Psi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}
,
U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}
,
\Gamma^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\delta\right)
,
\overline{\Gamma}^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)
,
T^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\delta\right)
,
\Theta^{k}_{\varepsilon}
and
P^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\delta\right)
be as in Section
3
. We define

R_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}:=U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}-\Delta^{-1}\left[\lambda_{\varepsilon}h_{\varepsilon}f\left(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}\right)\right].

(4.1)

As a first step, we obtain the following:

Proposition 4.1
.

Let
\varepsilon_{4}
be as in Proposition
3.6
. Assume that

\tfrac{3-\sqrt{5}}{4}<\delta_{0}<\tfrac{1}{2}\quad\text{and}\quad 0<\delta_{1}<3\delta_{0}-2\delta_{0}^{2}-\tfrac{1}{2}\,.

(4.2)

Then for every
\delta\in\left(0,1\right)
, there exist
\varepsilon_{5}\left(\delta\right)\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{4}\left(\delta\right)\right)
and
C_{5}=C_{5}\left(\delta\right)>0
such that

\left\|R_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}\right\|_{H^{1}_{0}}\leq C_{5}\frac{\delta_{\varepsilon}\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}

(4.3)

for all
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{5}\left(\delta\right)\right)
and
\left(\gamma,\tau,\theta\right)\in P^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\delta\right)
.

Proof.

For every
\psi\in H_{0}^{1}\left(\Omega\right)
, using that
B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\in V_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}
, integrating by parts and using (
3.20
), we obtain

\displaystyle\left<R_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta},\psi\right>_{H^{1}_{0}}

\displaystyle=\left<U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}-\Delta^{-1}\left[\lambda_{\varepsilon}h_{\varepsilon}f\left(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}\right)\right],\psi\right>_{H^{1}_{0}}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\big(\Delta U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}-\lambda_{\varepsilon}h_{\varepsilon}f\left(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}\right)\big)\psi dx

\displaystyle=\lambda_{\varepsilon}\int_{\Omega}\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(1+\theta_{i}\right)h_{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)f\left(\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)\mathbf{1}_{B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}\right)}+h_{\varepsilon}\chi_{\varepsilon,\tau}f\big(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\big)

\displaystyle\quad-h_{\varepsilon}f\left(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}\right)\bigg)\psi dx+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\theta_{i}\int_{\Omega}\psi\Delta B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}dx.

(4.4)

By using the definition of
\chi_{\varepsilon,\tau}
together with the mean value theorem, we obtain

\displaystyle\left|\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(1+\theta_{i}\right)h_{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)f\left(\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)\mathbf{1}_{B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}\right)}+h_{\varepsilon}\chi_{\varepsilon,\tau}f\big(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\big)-h_{\varepsilon}f\left(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}\right)\right|

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left(\left|h_{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)f\left(\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)-h_{\varepsilon}f\left(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}\right)\right|+\left|\theta_{i}\right|h_{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)f\left(\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)\right)\mathbf{1}_{B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}\right)}

\displaystyle\qquad+h_{\varepsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\big|f\big(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\big)\big|\mathbf{1}_{A\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon},r_{\varepsilon}+r_{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)}+h_{\varepsilon}\big|f\big(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\big)-f\left(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}\right)\big|\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{r_{\varepsilon},\tau}}

\displaystyle\quad\leq\sum_{i=1}^{k}\big(h_{\varepsilon}f^{\prime}\left(\left(1-t_{1}\right)\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}+t_{1}U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}\right)\left|U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}-\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right|

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\quad+\left|\nabla h_{\varepsilon}\left(\left(1-t_{2}\right)\overline{\tau_{i}}+t_{2}x\right)\right|\left|x-\overline{\tau_{i}}\right|f\left(\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)\big)\mathbf{1}_{B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}\right)}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+h_{\varepsilon}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\big|f\big(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\big)\big|\mathbf{1}_{A\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon},r_{\varepsilon}+r_{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)}+\bigg(h_{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)f\left(\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)

\displaystyle\quad\quad+h_{\varepsilon}f^{\prime}\bigg(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}+t_{3}\sum_{i=1}^{k}\theta_{i}B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\bigg)\bigg)\sum_{i=1}^{k}\left|\theta_{i}\right|B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{r_{\varepsilon},\tau}}

(4.5)

for some functions
t_{1},t_{2},t_{3}:\Omega\to\left[0,1\right]
, where
A\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon},r_{\varepsilon}+r_{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)
and
\Omega_{r_{\varepsilon},\tau}
are as in (
3.18
). Since
\lambda_{\varepsilon}\to\lambda_{0}
and
h_{\varepsilon}\to h_{0}
in
C^{1}\left(\overline{\Omega}\right)
, it follows from (
4
) and (
4
) that

\left<R_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta},\psi\right>_{H^{1}_{0}}=\bigO\bigg(\sum_{i=1}^{k}\int_{\Omega}\bigg(\big(f^{\prime}\left(\left(1-t_{1}\right)\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}+t_{1}U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}\right)\left|U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}-\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right|\\
+\left|x-\overline{\tau_{i}}\right|f\left(\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)\big)\mathbf{1}_{B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}\right)}+\big|f\big(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\big)\big|\mathbf{1}_{A\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon},r_{\varepsilon}+r_{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)}\\
+f^{\prime}\bigg(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}+t_{4}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\theta_{j}B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{j},\tau_{j}}\bigg)\left|\theta_{i}\right|B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{r_{\varepsilon},\tau}}\bigg)|\psi|dx\bigg).

(4.6)

For every
i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}
, by using (
3.34
) and remarking that
f^{\prime}\left(u\right)\leq 3uf\left(u\right)
for all
u\geq 1
, we obtain

\int_{B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}\right)}\left(f^{\prime}\left(\left(1-t_{1}\right)\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}+t_{1}U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}\right)\left|U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}-\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right|+\left|x-\overline{\tau_{i}}\right|f\left(\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)\right)\\
\times|\psi|dx=\bigO\bigg(\int_{B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}\right)}f\left(\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)\bigg(\frac{\delta_{\varepsilon}\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}+\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{1/l}\left|x-\overline{\tau_{i}}\right|\bigg)|\psi|dx\bigg).

(4.7)

By integrating by parts, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}\right)}f\left(\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)\left|\psi\right|dx

\displaystyle=\left(\lambda_{\varepsilon}h_{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)\right)^{-1}\left<B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}},\left|\psi\right|\right>_{H_{0}^{1}}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\lambda_{\varepsilon}h_{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)\right)^{-1}\left\|B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right\|_{H^{1}_{0}}\left\|\psi\right\|_{H^{1}_{0}}=\bigO\big(\left\|\psi\right\|_{H^{1}_{0}}\big).

(4.8)

On the other hand, for every
p>1
, by using Hölder’s inequality together with the continuity of the embedding
H^{1}_{0}\left(\Omega\right)\hookrightarrow L^{p^{\prime}}\left(\Omega\right)
, where
p^{\prime}
is the conjugate exponent of
p
, we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}\right)}f\left(\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)\left|x-\overline{\tau_{i}}\right|\left|\psi\right|dx=\bigO\big(\big\|f\left(\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)\left|x-\overline{\tau_{i}}\right|\mathbf{1}_{B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}\right)}\big\|_{L^{p}}\left\|\psi\right\|_{H^{1}_{0}}\big),

(4.9)

\displaystyle\int_{A\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon},r_{\varepsilon}+r_{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)}\big|f\big(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\big)\big|\left|\psi\right|dx=\bigO\big(\big\|f\big(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\big)\mathbf{1}_{A\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon},r_{\varepsilon}+r_{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)}\big\|_{L^{p}}\left\|\psi\right\|_{H^{1}_{0}}\big),

(4.10)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{r_{\varepsilon},\tau}}\bigg(f\left(\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)+f^{\prime}\bigg(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}+t_{4}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\theta_{j}B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{j},\tau_{j}}\bigg)B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\bigg)\left|\psi\right|dx

\displaystyle\quad=\bigO\bigg(\bigg\|\bigg(f\left(\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)+f^{\prime}\bigg(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}+t_{4}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\theta_{j}B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{j},\tau_{j}}\bigg)B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\bigg)\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{r_{\varepsilon},\tau}}\bigg\|_{L^{p}}\left\|\psi\right\|_{H^{1}_{0}}\bigg).

(4.11)

By rescaling, we obtain

\big\|f\left(\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)\left|x-\overline{\tau_{i}}\right|\mathbf{1}_{B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}\right)}\big\|_{L^{p}}^{p}=\mu_{i,\varepsilon}^{p+2}\int_{B\left(0,r_{\varepsilon}/\mu_{i,\varepsilon}\right)}f\left(\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}+\mu_{i,\varepsilon}x\right)\right)^{p}\left|x\right|^{p}dx,

where
\mu_{i,\varepsilon}
is defined by
\mu_{i,\varepsilon}^{2}:=4\gamma_{i,\varepsilon}^{-2}\exp\left(-\gamma_{i,\varepsilon}^{2}\right)
. By using (
3.10
) and (
6.2
), it follows that

\displaystyle\big\|f\left(\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)\left|x-\overline{\tau_{i}}\right|\mathbf{1}_{B\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon}\right)}\big\|_{L^{p}}^{p}

\displaystyle=\bigO\left(\mu_{i,\varepsilon}^{p+2}\int_{B\left(0,r_{\varepsilon}/\mu_{i,\varepsilon}\right)}f\left(\gamma_{i,\varepsilon}-\frac{1}{\gamma_{i,\varepsilon}}\ln\frac{1}{1+\lambda_{\varepsilon}h_{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)\left|x\right|^{2}}\right)^{p}\left|x\right|^{p}dx\right)

\displaystyle=\bigO\left(\frac{\mu_{i,\varepsilon}^{2-p}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{p}}\int_{B\left(0,r_{\varepsilon}/\mu_{i,\varepsilon}\right)}\frac{\left|x\right|^{p}dx}{\big(1+\lambda_{\varepsilon}h_{\varepsilon}\left(\overline{\tau_{i}}\right)\left|x\right|^{2}\big)^{2p}}\right)=\bigO\left(\frac{\mu_{i,\varepsilon}^{2-p}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{p}}\right)=\smallo\left(\left(\frac{\delta_{\varepsilon}\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2+1/l}}\right)^{p}\right)

(4.12)

provided we choose
p
such that
2-p>p\left(\delta_{1}+1/2\right)
, i.e.
1<p<4/\left(2\delta_{1}+3\right)
, which is possible since
\delta_{1}<3\delta_{0}-2\delta_{0}^{2}-1/2<1/2
. As regards the terms in the right-hand sides of (
4.10
) and (
4.11
), by using (
3.10
) and (
5.11
) and proceeding as in (
5.12
)–(
5.15
) and (
5.29
), we obtain

\displaystyle\big\|f\big(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\big)\mathbf{1}_{A\left(\overline{\tau_{i}},r_{\varepsilon},r_{\varepsilon}+r_{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)}\big\|^{p}_{L^{p}}=\bigO\left(\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{p}\ln\left(1+r_{\varepsilon}\right)\exp\left(\left(p\delta_{0}-1\right)\delta_{0}\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\smallo\left(\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}\right)\right)\right)

\displaystyle\qquad=\bigO\left(\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{p}\exp\left(\left(p\delta_{0}-\frac{3}{2}\right)\delta_{0}\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\smallo\left(\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)\right)\right)=\smallo\left(\left(\frac{\delta_{\varepsilon}\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}\right)^{p}\right),

(4.13)

\displaystyle\bigg\|\bigg(f\left(\overline{B}_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right)+f^{\prime}\bigg(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}+t_{4}\sum_{j=1}^{k}\theta_{j}B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{j},\tau_{j}}\bigg)B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\bigg)\mathbf{1}_{\Omega_{r_{\varepsilon},\tau}}\bigg\|_{L^{p}}^{p}

\displaystyle\qquad=\bigO\Bigg(\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{3p+2}\exp\left(\left(p\delta_{0}-1\right)\delta_{0}\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}+\smallo\left(\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}\right)\right)+\frac{1}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{p}}\int_{\Omega_{R_{\varepsilon},\tau}}\left|\ln\left|x-\overline{\tau_{i}}\right|+\bigO\left(1\right)\right|^{p}dx\Bigg)

\displaystyle\qquad=\smallo\left(1\right)

(4.14)

as
\varepsilon\to 0
, uniformly in
\left(\gamma,\tau,\theta\right)\in P^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\delta\right)
, provided we choose
p
such that

\left(p\delta_{0}-\frac{3}{2}\right)\delta_{0}<-\frac{p}{2}\left(\delta_{1}+\frac{1}{2}\right)\quad\text{and}\quad p\delta_{0}-1<0,\\
\text{i.e. }1<p<\min\left(\frac{3\delta_{0}}{2\delta_{0}^{2}+\delta_{1}+1/2},\frac{1}{\delta_{0}}\right)=\frac{3\delta_{0}}{2\delta_{0}^{2}+\delta_{1}+1/2}\,,

which is possible when assuming (
4.2
). By putting together (
4.6
)–(
4.14
) and using the fact that
\left|\theta_{i}\right|<\delta_{\varepsilon}\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{-4}\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}
, we obtain (
4.3
). This ends the proof of Proposition
4.1
.
∎

We let
\mathcal{H}
be the vector space of all functions in
H^{1}_{0}\left(\Omega\right)
that are even in
x_{2}
. For every
\tau\in T^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\delta\right)
and
\gamma\in\overline{\Gamma}^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\tau\right)
, we define

{V_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}}:=\text{span}\left\{Z_{0,i,\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau},Z_{1,i,\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau},B_{\varepsilon,\gamma_{i},\tau_{i}}\right\}_{1\leq i\leq k},

where

Z_{0,i,\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}:=\partial_{\gamma_{i}}\left[U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,0}\right]\quad\text{and}\quad Z_{1,i,\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}:=\partial_{\tau_{i}}\left[U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,0}\right]\quad\forall i\in\left\{1,\dotsc,k\right\}.

Note that
U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,0}\in\mathcal{H}
and
V_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}\subset\mathcal{H}
. We let
\Pi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}
and
\Pi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}^{\perp}
be the orthogonal projection of
\mathcal{H}
onto
V_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}
and
V_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}^{\perp}
, respectively. We obtain the following:

Proposition 4.2
.

Assume that (
4.2
) holds true. Let
\varepsilon_{5}
be as in Proposition
4.1
. Then for every
\delta\in\left(0,1\right)
, there exist
\varepsilon_{6}\left(\delta\right)\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{5}\left(\delta\right)\right)
and
C_{6}=C_{6}\left(\delta\right)>0
such that for every
\varepsilon\in\left(0,\varepsilon_{6}\left(\delta\right)\right)
and
\left(\gamma,\tau,\theta\right)\in P^{k}_{\varepsilon}\left(\delta\right)
, there exists a unique solution
\Phi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}\in V_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}^{\perp}
to the equation

\Pi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau}^{\perp}\left(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}+\Phi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}-\Delta^{-1}\left[\lambda_{\varepsilon}h_{\varepsilon}f\left(U_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}+\Phi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}\right)\right]\right)=0

(4.15)

such that

\left\|\Phi_{\varepsilon,\gamma,\tau,\theta}\right\|_{H^{1}_{0}}\leq C_{6}\frac{\delta_{\varepsilon}\ln\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}}{\overline{\gamma}_{\varepsilon}^{2}}.

(4.16)

Furthermore,
